- **3.2.1** (MUST NOT) `rfc8277#3.2.1-no-multilabel-propagation-without-capability` - not-applicable
  > Note that a given route MUST NOT be propagated to a given peer if the route's NLRI has multiple labels, but the Multiple Labels Capability was not negotiated with the peer.
  Propagation again, and for the same reason: exabgp has no adj-rib-out fed from an
adj-rib-in.  The closely related obligation which DOES bind exabgp, that it must not
originate a multi label NLRI towards a peer which never sent the capability, is recorded
as rfc8277#2.1-must-not-send-multiple-labels-uncapable and is a gap rather than
not-applicable.
- **3.2.3** (MUST NOT) `rfc8277#3.2.3-must-not-send-more-labels-than-peer-handles` - proven
  > A BGP speaker MUST NOT send multiple labels to a peer with which it has not exchanged the Multiple Labels Capability and MUST NOT send more labels to a given peer than the peer has announced (via the Multiple Labels Capability) than it can handle.
  `Negotiated.labels_limit` is the Count the peer's first triple for the family gave, when
both OPENs carried code 8, and 1 otherwise; `pack_nlri` never sends more labels than that.
A stack within the count goes out whole. Positive only: this binds what we send, and no input from a peer can violate it.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8277_labelled_unicast.py::test_we_send_no_more_labels_than_the_peer_said_it_handles`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8277_labelled_unicast.py::test_a_stack_within_what_the_peer_handles_goes_out_whole`

## rfc8654

- **4** (MUST) `rfc8654#4-receive-up-to-65535-when-advertised` - proven
  > An implementation that advertises the BGP Extended Message Capability MUST be capable of receiving a message with a length up to and including 65,535 octets.
  Until 2026-10-08 the limit on receipt rose only when both sides had advertised the
capability, so a peer which used our advertisement without advertising its own was
refused, and a session with no local-as (mirroring the peer's AS) copied the limit before
its own OPEN was sent and kept 4096 octets for its life.  Both are tested, with and
without the peer's capability.  The negative test is a session which did not advertise
it, whose limit stays 4096.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8654_extended_message.py::test_advertising_the_capability_raises_what_we_receive`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8654_extended_message.py::test_not_advertising_the_capability_keeps_what_we_receive_to_4096`
- **4** (MAY) `rfc8654#4-send-only-if-received` - untested (missing: negative)
  > A BGP speaker MAY send BGP Extended Messages to a peer only if the BGP Extended Message Capability was received from that peer.
  Negotiated.msg_size, the size UPDATEs are split to, is 65535 only when the peer's OPEN
carried the capability.  It also asks for ours to have been sent: the MAY is declined
towards a peer we told we could not take them, which is the conservative reading and the
behaviour exabgp always had.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8654_extended_message.py::test_what_we_send_is_extended_only_with_their_capability`
- **5** (MUST NOT) `rfc8654#5-not-advertised-not-accepted` - proven
  > A BGP speaker that has the ability to use BGP Extended Messages but has not advertised the BGP Extended Message Capability, presumably due to configuration, MUST NOT accept a BGP Extended Message.
  Without our capability the connection's limit stays 4096 octets, whatever the peer
advertised, and a longer message is refused by Connection.reader_async with Bad Message
Length (RFC 4271 6.1), as section 4 says the listener does.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8654_extended_message.py::test_not_advertising_the_capability_keeps_what_we_receive_to_4096`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8654_extended_message.py::test_an_extended_message_we_did_not_advertise_for_is_a_bad_message_length`

## rfc8669

- **3** (MUST) `rfc8669#3-unknown-tlv-ignored-and-propagated` - proven
  > For future extensibility, unknown TLVs MUST be ignored and propagated unmodified.
  `PrefixSid.unpack_attribute` wraps a TLV type it does not know in `GenericSRId`, which
keeps the raw value, and the attribute keeps the peer's bytes in `_packed` so
`pack_attribute` re-emits the whole attribute exactly as it arrived.  The negative side
is the one which matters: an unknown TLV must not make the attribute malformed, and the
bytes which come back out must be byte-for-byte what came in, not a re-encoding.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_prefix_sid_holding_only_an_unknown_tlv_still_decodes`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_unknown_tlv_is_re_emitted_byte_for_byte_beside_a_known_one`
- **3.1** (MUST) `rfc8669#3.1-flags-ignored-on-reception` - proven
  > Flags: 16 bits of flags. None are defined by this document. The Flags field MUST be clear on transmission and MUST be ignored on reception.
  Same shape as RESERVED and enforced by the same code: `make_labelindex` writes zero
flags, and the decoder never reads bytes 1 to 3 of the value.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_label_index_with_zero_flags_decodes`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_every_flag_bit_set_neither_refuses_the_tlv_nor_changes_the_index`
- **3.1** (MUST) `rfc8669#3.1-label-index-must-be-present` - proven
  > The Label-Index TLV MUST be present in the BGP Prefix-SID attribute attached to IPv4/IPv6 Labeled Unicast prefixes ([RFC8277]). It MUST be ignored when received for other BGP AFI/SAFI combinations.
  Checked where the family exists, after the NLRI are built:
`UpdateCollection._without_invalid_prefix_sid`, at the end of `_parse_payload`. When the
UPDATE announces labelled unicast (SAFI 4) and its Prefix-SID holds no Label-Index TLV,
the attribute is discarded (section 6: ignored, not propagated) on a copy of the
collection, which may be the session's cached one, and the routes are kept. On any other
family the Label-Index is ignored, as the second sentence asks: nothing looks at it.
One UPDATE carries one attribute set, so a mixed UPDATE is judged as a whole: labelled
unicast anywhere in it applies the rule.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_labelled_unicast_route_whose_prefix_sid_has_no_label_index_loses_the_attribute`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_prefix_sid_with_a_label_index_tlv_is_accepted`
- **3.1** (MUST) `rfc8669#3.1-reserved-ignored-on-reception` - proven
  > RESERVED: 8-bit field. It MUST be clear on transmission and MUST be ignored on reception.
  `SrLabelIndex.make_labelindex` packs the RESERVED octet as zero, and the `labelindex`
property reads only bytes 3 to 7, so a non-zero RESERVED changes nothing on the way in.
The negative test is the useful one: a decoder which refused a non-zero RESERVED, or
which let it bleed into the label index, would fail it.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_label_index_with_a_zero_reserved_octet_decodes_to_its_index`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_non_zero_reserved_octet_neither_refuses_the_tlv_nor_changes_the_index`
- **3.2** (MUST) `rfc8669#3.2-srgb-flags-ignored-on-reception` - proven
  > Flags: 16 bits of flags. None are defined in this document. The Flags field MUST be clear on transmission and MUST be ignored on reception.
  `SrGb.srgbs` skips the two flag bytes and walks whole six byte entries, so a non-zero
Flags field is invisible to everything downstream.  `make_srgb` packs zero.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_originator_srgb_decodes_its_ranges`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_non_zero_srgb_flags_do_not_shift_the_first_range`
- **3.2** (MUST NOT) `rfc8669#3.2-srgb-not-changed-in-propagation` - proven
  > The Originator SRGB TLV MUST NOT be changed during the propagation of the BGP update.
  `PrefixSid.unpack_attribute` keeps the peer's bytes as `original` and `pack_attribute`
returns them, so nothing exabgp propagates is a re-encoding.  The negative side is a
concatenated multi-range SRGB, which a decoder that rebuilt the TLV from its parsed
ranges could reorder or merge without anyone noticing.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_single_range_srgb_is_packed_back_as_it_arrived`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_three_concatenated_srgb_ranges_keep_their_order_and_their_bytes`
- **4** (MUST) `rfc8669#4-ebgp-outside-sr-domain-discard` - proven
  > A BGP speaker receiving a BGP Prefix-SID attribute from an External BGP (EBGP) neighbor residing outside the boundaries of the SR domain MUST discard the attribute unless it is configured to accept the attribute from the EBGP neighbor.
  "Residing outside the boundaries of the SR domain" is knowledge exabgp does not have and
has no way to acquire: it has no notion of an SR domain, no domain membership
configuration, and no data plane whose integrity the boundary is protecting.  Every
session is therefore one the operator configured to accept the attribute, which is the
escape clause this sentence itself provides.  What exabgp owes is that the attribute
reaches the API as it arrived, so the operator can act on it, and that is what is tested.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_the_attribute_reaches_the_api_unfiltered_for_the_operator_to_judge`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_the_attribute_reaches_the_api_unfiltered_for_the_operator_to_judge`
- **4.1** (MUST) `rfc8669#4.1-no-label-index-is-invalid` - proven
  > A BGP Prefix-SID attribute received without a Label-Index TLV MUST be considered to be "invalid" by the receiving speaker.
  The same pass as rfc8669#3.1-label-index-must-be-present: an attribute with no Label-Index
TLV on a labelled unicast UPDATE is invalid, so it is discarded and the routes kept.
Section 4.1 is "MPLS Data Plane: Labeled Unicast", and RFC 9252 puts SRv6 service TLVs
with no Label-Index in this attribute on VPN and EVPN routes, so the rule is not applied
to any other family; a test holds that door shut.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_prefix_sid_carrying_only_an_srgb_is_treated_as_invalid`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_labelled_unicast_prefix_sid_with_a_label_index_is_kept`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_prefix_sid_carrying_a_label_index_is_not_treated_as_invalid`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_srv6_service_attribute_without_a_label_index_stays_valid`
- **6** (SHALL) `rfc8669#6-duplicate-attribute-first-wins` - proven
  > As per [RFC7606], if the BGP Prefix-SID attribute appears more than once in an UPDATE message, all the occurrences of the attribute other than the first one SHALL be discarded and the UPDATE message will continue to be processed.
  `AttributeCollection.parse` skips an attribute code it already holds when the class does
not set `NO_DUPLICATE`, and `PrefixSid` does not set it.  The negative side is what the
second half of the sentence asks for: the rest of the UPDATE must survive, so an
attribute which follows the duplicate still has to be decoded.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_the_second_prefix_sid_attribute_is_dropped_and_the_first_kept`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_attribute_after_the_duplicate_is_still_decoded`
- **6** (SHALL) `rfc8669#6-duplicate-tlv-first-wins` - proven
  > Similarly, if a recognized TLV appears more than once in a BGP Prefix-SID attribute while the specification only allows for a single occurrence, then all the occurrences of the TLV other than the first one SHALL be discarded and the Prefix-SID attribute will continue to be processed.
  `PrefixSid.unpack_attribute` keeps the first Label-Index and the first Originator SRGB and
discards any later occurrence of either, which are the only two TLVs this document defines
and neither may repeat.  Discarded means gone: the repeat is dropped from `sr_attrs`, so
the JSON carries one `sr-label-index` key, and it is dropped from the bytes re-advertised
too, since this section promises propagation to unknown TLVs alone.  That is deliberately
not what RFC 9012 13 asks for a repeated sub-TLV, which stays on the wire.  The TLVs which
are kept keep the peer's own framing: the attribute is rebuilt from the original slices
rather than from the decoded objects.  Recorded positive-only:
the negative side would be "a TLV type which may legitimately repeat is not collapsed",
and this document defines no such TLV, so there is nothing to test against.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_repeated_label_index_tlv_keeps_only_the_first`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_discarded_repeat_is_not_advertised_onwards_but_its_neighbours_are`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_repeated_originator_srgb_keeps_only_the_first`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_repeated_unknown_tlv_is_left_alone`
- **6** (MUST) `rfc8669#6-malformed-attribute-discard` - proven
  > When a BGP speaker receives a BGP UPDATE message containing a malformed or invalid BGP Prefix-SID attribute attached to an IPv4/ IPv6 Labeled Unicast prefix ([RFC8277]), it MUST ignore the received BGP Prefix-SID attribute and not advertise it to other BGP peers. In this context, a malformed BGP Prefix-SID attribute is one that cannot be parsed due to not meeting the minimum attribute length requirement, containing a TLV length that doesn't conform to the length constraints for the TLV, or containing a TLV length that would extend beyond the end of the attribute (as defined by the attribute length).
  `PrefixSid.DISCARD = True` covers all three malformed shapes this sentence names.  A TLV
length which does not conform to the TLV's constraints and a TLV length which runs past
the end of the attribute reach `AttributeCollection.parse` as a `Notify` and become an
`INTERNAL_DISCARD`.  The third, a Prefix-SID attribute of length zero, which is precisely
"not meeting the minimum attribute length requirement", used to be caught earlier by the
generic `length == 0 and not kls.VALID_ZERO` rule and became `INTERNAL_TREAT_AS_WITHDRAW`,
withdrawing the route the RFC says to keep.  `PrefixSid` now sets `VALID_ZERO` so that
generic rule does not answer for it, and refuses an empty value in `unpack_attribute`
instead, which `DISCARD` turns into the discard section 6 asks for.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_tlv_length_outside_the_tlv_constraint_is_discarded_not_withdrawn`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_tlv_running_past_the_end_of_the_attribute_is_discarded`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_srgb_which_is_not_two_plus_a_multiple_of_six_is_discarded`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_an_attribute_below_the_minimum_length_is_discarded_and_the_route_kept`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8669_prefix_sid.py::test_a_well_formed_prefix_sid_is_not_discarded`

## rfc8950

- **3** (MUST) `rfc8950#3-length-determines-next-hop-protocol` - proven
  > The BGP speaker receiving the advertisement MUST use the Length of Next Hop Address field to determine which network-layer protocol the next-hop address belongs to.
  `next_hop_lengths` in protocol/family.py gives the lengths a family takes on a session:
its own, and 16 or 32 (24 or 48 behind an RD) for an IPv4 <AFI, SAFI> which negotiated an
IPv6 next hop, for that <AFI, SAFI> only.  The decoder used to switch every family to the
table of the AFI the length suggested once any entry was negotiated: a family never
negotiated took an IPv6 next hop, and EVPN, VPLS and BGP-LS raised KeyError.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8950_extended_next_hop.py::test_the_length_says_which_protocol_the_next_hop_is`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8950_extended_next_hop.py::test_an_ipv6_next_hop_needs_the_capability_for_that_family`
- **4** (MUST) `rfc8950#4-advertise-only-after-capability` - proven
  > A BGP speaker MUST only advertise the IPv4 or VPN-IPv4 NLRI with an IPv6 next hop to a BGP peer if the BGP speaker has first ascertained via the BGP Capability Advertisement that the BGP peer supports the Extended Next Hop Encoding capability for the relevant AFI/SAFI pair.
  `MPNLRICollection.packed_reach_attributes` leaves out, with a warning, an IPv4 route of
SAFI 1, 2, 4 or 128 whose next hop is IPv6 unless that <AFI, SAFI> negotiated an IPv6 next
hop.  The configuration refuses such a route before it is sent; this is what holds for an
API announcement, or a peer which withheld the capability.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8950_extended_next_hop.py::test_an_ipv4_route_goes_out_with_an_ipv6_next_hop_once_the_capability_allows_it`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8950_extended_next_hop.py::test_an_ipv4_route_with_an_ipv6_next_hop_is_not_sent_without_the_capability`

## rfc8955

- **4** (MUST) `rfc8955#4-capability-multiprotocol` - proven
  > Implementations wishing to exchange Flow Specification MUST use BGP's Capability Advertisement facility to exchange the Multiprotocol Extension Capability Code (Code 1), as defined in [RFC4760]. The (AFI, SAFI) pair carried in the Multiprotocol Extension Capability MUST be (AFI=1, SAFI=133) for IPv4 Flow Specification and (AFI=1, SAFI=134) for VPNv4 Flow Specification.
  `SAFI.FLOW_IP` is 133 and `SAFI.FLOW_VPN` is 134, and the flow families go into the
MULTIPROTOCOL capability like every other.  The negative side is the one which matters on
the wire: an MP_REACH_NLRI for 1/133 from a peer which never advertised the capability is
refused by `MPRNLRI.unpack_attribute`, so the capability is a precondition and not a
decoration.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_flow_specification_safis_are_133_and_134`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_specification_for_a_family_never_advertised_is_refused`
- **4** (MUST) `rfc8955#4-next-hop-ignored` - proven
  > The Network Address of the Next-Hop field MUST be ignored.
  exabgp runs no next-hop resolution and no feasibility test, so nothing it does with a
flow specification depends on this field.  `Family.size` lists 0 alongside 4 for
(ipv4, flow_ip) and 0 alongside 16 and 32 for (ipv6, flow_ip), which is what lets a
next-hop sent by a peer which does not follow the sentence above be accepted and then
disregarded.  The negative side is that the value in the field cannot change the NLRI
which comes out.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_next_hop_sent_by_a_peer_does_not_change_the_flow_specification`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_an_unusable_next_hop_does_not_make_a_flow_specification_unusable`
- **4** (MUST) `rfc8955#4-next-hop-length-zero` - proven
  > When advertising Flow Specifications, the Length of the Next-Hop Network Address MUST be set to 0.
  `MPNLRICollection._encode_nexthop` writes a next hop length of 0 for SAFI 133 and 134,
whatever next hop the route was given. The one exception is a route carrying the
draft-simpson-idr-flowspec-redirect-ip community (0x08/0x00), which exists to point at
the next hop and is only sent when the operator writes `redirect-simpson` or
`redirect-to-nexthop-simpson`; `UpdateCollection.messages` hands the route's attributes to
the collection so it can tell.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_specification_is_advertised_with_a_next_hop_length_of_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_next_hop_on_a_flow_route_is_not_put_on_the_wire`
- **4.2** (MAY) `rfc8955#4.2-component-once` - proven
  > A given component type MAY (exactly once) be present in the Flow Specification.
  "Exactly once" used to be the half exabgp lost, and losing it inverted the filter rather
than widening it.  Two type 3 components must be ANDed, because section 4.2 says a packet
matches the intersection of all the components present, so "protocol == tcp" followed by
"protocol == udp" matches nothing.  `Flow._parse_rules` appended the second component's
operations to the first component's list, where the second operator's AND bit is unset, so
the two became "protocol tcp OR udp" and the rule matched both.
`Flow._check_component_order` now refuses a repeated component type as a treat-as-withdraw.

A repeated type 1 or type 2 prefix used to be exempt on both sides: `Flow.add` let the
configuration and the API write several destination or source prefixes in one flow, so
`destination 10.0.0.0/8; destination 11.0.0.0/8;` went out as `01080A 01080B`, and the
decoder accepted it back.  `rule_conflict` now refuses the second prefix with an error
naming both, and the decoder treats a repeated prefix as it treats any repeated component.
The same code serves RFC 8956, which keeps this encoding for IPv6.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_component_type_present_once_decodes`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_we_never_encode_a_second_destination_prefix`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_configuration_refuses_a_second_prefix_of_one_type`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_api_refuses_a_second_prefix_of_one_type`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_component_type_present_twice_is_refused`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_repeated_destination_or_source_prefix_is_refused`
- **4.2** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2-precede-higher-type` - proven
  > If present, it MUST precede any component of higher numeric type value.
  The same check as rfc8955#4.2-strict-type-ordering, stated from the other end, and kept
separate because a decoder could be written to enforce one and not the other: a sender
could emit types 1, 4, 3 and satisfy "each type appears once" while still putting a type 4
in front of a type 3.  `Flow._check_component_order` compares each component type against
the one before it, so that sequence is refused on the 3.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_lower_type_before_a_higher_one_decodes`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_higher_type_before_a_lower_one_is_refused`
- **4.2** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2-strict-type-ordering` - proven
  > Components MUST follow strict type ordering by increasing numerical order.
  exabgp's encoder honours this: `Flow._pack_from_rules` walks `sorted(self.rules.keys())`.
Its decoder checks it as the components are read, in `Flow._check_component_order`, which
is the only place that can: the rules land in a dict keyed by type and a dict cannot show
what order they arrived in.  A component whose type is lower than the one before it is a
treat-as-withdraw, per section 10 and RFC 7606.  Before the check, a flow specification
arriving in decreasing order was accepted and re-rendered in the right order, so exabgp's
API reported a filter the peer had not sent.  The dead `seen` list which used to sit in
`Flow.unpack_nlri`, built from `sorted(rules.keys())` and never compared to anything, is
gone with it.

One deviation is deliberate: a repeated type 1 or type 2 prefix is allowed through, so
equal consecutive types are refused for every component except those two.  `Flow.add` has
accepted several source or destination prefixes from the configuration for years because
some vendors send them, and a decoder which refused what this encoder emits could not read
exabgp's own output back.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_components_in_increasing_type_order_decode`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_components_in_decreasing_type_order_are_refused`
- **4.2** (SHOULD NOT) `rfc8955#4.2-unmatchable-not-propagated` - proven
  > However, some combinations cannot match any packets (e.g., "ICMP Type AND Port" will never match any packets) and thus SHOULD NOT be propagated by BGP.
  `Flow.unmatchable` names the section's own example, an ICMP type or code with a port
(ICMP has no ports), and the configuration and the API drop such a route with a warning
(`flow.unmatchable`) rather than announce it. The configuration still loads, so a file
which carried one before keeps working without it. Other unmatchable combinations are not
looked for. Positive only: this binds what we send, and no input from a peer can violate it.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_specification_matching_icmp_type_and_port_is_not_propagated`
- **4.2.1.1** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.1.1-and-bit-first-unset` - proven
  > In the first operator octet of a sequence, it MUST be encoded as unset and MUST be treated as always unset on decoding.
  The encoding half holds: the configuration grammar only sets the AND bit on an operation
which follows another inside one component.  The decoding half holds too:
`Flow._parse_operations` clears bit 0x40 on the first operation of every component, so a
first operator octet of 0xC1 renders as `=tcp` rather than as `&=tcp`, an AND against a
predecessor which does not exist.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_first_operator_octet_we_encode_has_the_and_bit_clear`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_an_and_bit_in_the_first_operator_octet_is_treated_as_unset`
- **4.2.1.1** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.1.1-reserved-bit-zero` - proven
  > 0: MUST be set to 0 on NLRI encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding
  Bit 0x08 of the numeric operator octet.  exabgp never sets it when encoding, because
`NumericOperator` builds the low nibble out of LT, GT and EQ only.  On decoding it is
masked off: `NumericOperator.OPERATOR` is the AND bit and the three comparison bits, so an
octet of 0x89 decodes as the plain equality 0x81 and a protocol match for TCP is reported
to the API as `protocol =tcp` whether the reserved bit arrived set or clear.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_no_numeric_operator_we_encode_sets_the_reserved_bit`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_reserved_bit_of_a_numeric_operator_is_ignored_on_decoding`
- **4.2.1.2** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.1.2-bitmask-reserved-bits-zero` - proven
  > 0 (all 0 bits): MUST be set to 0 on NLRI encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding
  Bits 0x0C of the bitmask operator octet, one more than the numeric operator reserves.
Encoding never sets them and `BinaryOperator.OPERATOR`, which is the AND bit and the NOT
and Match bits, drops them on decoding, so a fragment match arrives at the API as
`=dont-fragment+first-fragment` with the reserved bits set or clear.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_no_bitmask_operator_we_encode_sets_the_reserved_bits`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_reserved_bits_of_a_bitmask_operator_are_ignored_on_decoding`
- **4.2.2.11** (SHOULD) `rfc8955#4.2.2.11-dscp-other-bits-zero` - untested (missing: positive)
  > The six least significant bits contain the DSCP value. All other bits SHOULD be treated as 0.
  `FlowDSCP.decoder` masks the value with 0x3F, so the two high bits of the octet (the ECN
field of the IP header) are ignored on receipt; the configuration refuses a DSCP above 63,
so they are always zero on the way out.  RFC 8956 3 says type 11 applies to IPv6 "as
defined in [RFC8955]", with no keyword of its own, so the sentence binds `FlowTrafficClass`
too: until 2026-10-08 it decoded the whole octet, and 030b81c1 was traffic-class =193.  It
uses the same decoder now, and both families are tested.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_two_high_bits_of_a_dscp_octet_are_ignored_on_decoding`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_two_high_bits_of_an_ipv6_traffic_class_octet_are_ignored_on_decoding`
- **4.2.2.11** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.2.11-dscp-single-octet` - proven
  > Type 11 component values MUST be encoded as single octet (numeric_op len=00).
  `FlowDSCP` derives from `IOperationByte`, whose `encode` always returns one octet.  The
negative side is `dscp_value` in the same file, which refuses anything above 63 at
configuration time, so no value which could not fit a single octet ever reaches the
encoder.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_every_dscp_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_dscp_which_would_not_fit_a_single_octet_is_refused_by_the_grammar`
- **4.2.2.12** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.2.12-fragment-reserved-bits-zero` - proven
  > 0: MUST be set to 0 on NLRI encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding
  The four high bits of the fragment bitmask value, not of the operator octet.  exabgp
encodes them as zero, since `Fragment` defines nothing above 0x0F.  They were not ignored
on decoding: `Fragment.named` reports an unknown bit rather than dropping it, so a value
of 0xF5 was rendered as `dont-fragment+first-fragment+unknown fragment type 245`.  The
decoder of `FlowFragment` now masks the value with the four bits this section defines, so
0xF5 and 0x05 decode to the same filter.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_no_fragment_bitmask_we_encode_sets_a_reserved_bit`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_reserved_bits_of_a_fragment_bitmask_are_ignored_on_decoding`
- **4.2.2.12** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.2.12-fragment-single-octet` - proven
  > The Type 12 component bitmask MUST be encoded as single octet bitmask (bitmask_op len=00).
  Held, but by accident rather than by construction: `FlowFragment` derives from
`IOperationByteShort`, which would emit two octets for a value of 256 or more, and what
keeps it to one is that every name `Fragment` defines is 0x0F or below.  The negative test
is therefore the one worth having, because it pins the property the class does not state.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_every_fragment_bitmask_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_no_fragment_value_could_need_a_second_octet`
- **4.2.2.9** (MUST) `rfc8955#4.2.2.9-tcp-flags-width` - proven
  > Type 9 component bitmasks MUST be encoded as 1- or 2-octet bitmask (bitmask_op len=00 or len=01).
  `FlowTCPFlag` derives from `IOperationByteShort`, whose `encode` returns one octet below
256 and two above, so the class cannot emit a four or eight octet bitmask.  The negative
side is the configuration boundary.  The grammar takes numbers as well as flag names, and
it accepted any, so 65536 would have needed a third octet; `FlowTCPFlag.converter` now
refuses anything above 0x0FFF.  That is tighter than two octets on purpose: two octets
match TCP header octets 13 and 14 "with the data offset (leftmost 4 bits) always treated as
0", so a bitmask with one of those four bits set matches no packet.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_every_tcp_flag_we_encode_uses_one_or_two_octets`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_tcp_flags_bitmask_below_the_data_offset_is_one_or_two_octets`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_no_tcp_flag_value_could_need_a_third_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_tcp_flags_bitmask_reaching_the_data_offset_is_refused_by_the_grammar`
- **6** (MUST) `rfc8955#6-enforce-leftmost-as` - proven
  > BGP implementations MUST also enforce that the AS_PATH attribute of a route received via the External Border Gateway Protocol (eBGP) contains the neighboring AS in the left-most position of the AS_PATH attribute.
  `UpdateCollection._not_first_as_of_peer` treats as withdrawn (RFC 7606, for the malformed
AS_PATH of RFC 4271 6.3) any route from an EBGP neighbour, outside the confederation, whose
AS_PATH does not start with an AS_SEQUENCE led by the peer's AS, logged as
update.first-as.withdraw. Every family, not only flow routes. A route server does not
prepend its AS, so `enforce-first-as false` on its neighbour turns the check off; the
default is on. A neighbouring Member-AS is held to RFC 5065 instead.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_leftmost_as_rule_does_not_bind_an_internal_peer`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_an_ebgp_route_whose_as_path_does_not_start_with_the_peer_as_is_not_accepted`
- **6** (MUST) `rfc8955#6-revalidate-on-unicast-change` - proven
  > Therefore, a revalidation of the Flow Specification NLRI MUST be performed whenever unicast routes change.
  Whenever an UPDATE changes the peer's IPv4 or IPv6 unicast routes, `validate_flows` judges
every flow of the peer again: one held in the adj-rib-in which is no longer feasible is
removed and held back, one held back which has become feasible is put in the adj-rib-in,
and each change is told to the API as an UPDATE of its own (Update.from_collection) after
the one which caused it. A flow the peer withdraws while held back is forgotten.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_specification_is_no_longer_feasible_once_its_unicast_route_is_withdrawn`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_held_back_becomes_feasible_when_its_unicast_route_arrives_and_the_api_is_told`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_withdrawn_flow_is_not_brought_back_by_a_later_unicast_route`
- **6** (MAY) `rfc8955#6-rule-a-may-be-relaxed` - untested (missing: negative)
  > However, rule a MAY be relaxed by explicit configuration, permitting Flow Specifications that include no destination prefix component.
  `flow-validation relaxed` is the explicit configuration: a flow specification with no
destination prefix component is feasible, and rules b and c are not applied to it. A flow
with a destination is still judged by all three.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_relaxed_validation_accepts_a_flow_with_no_destination`
- **6** (MUST) `rfc8955#6-rules-b-and-c-disregarded` - proven
  > If such is the case, rules b and c are moot and MUST be disregarded.
  Only a flow with no destination component, and only with `flow-validation relaxed`, skips
rules b and c. With `flow-validation enable` such a flow fails rule a; with a destination,
rules b and c apply in both modes. Off by default, as rfc8955#6-validation-feasible-if-and-
only-if records.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_rules_b_and_c_are_not_disregarded_without_explicit_configuration`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_relaxed_validation_still_applies_rules_b_and_c_to_a_flow_with_a_destination`
- **6** (MUST) `rfc8955#6-validation-feasible-if-and-only-if` - proven
  > In the absence of explicit configuration, a Flow Specification NLRI MUST be validated such that it is considered feasible if and only if all of the conditions below are true:
  Implemented in exabgp/rib/flow_validation.py, and OFF by default: `flow-validation enable`
on the neighbour turns it on. That is a deliberate departure from "in the absence of
explicit configuration", decided for this release because many exabgp users receive flow
specifications with no unicast route behind them (controllers, DDoS feeds) and would lose
them all on upgrade; the ledger records the rule as implemented because it is, and this
note records that the default is not the one the RFC asks for.

When on, `validate_flows` runs in Protocol.read_message, before the API or the adj-rib-in
see the UPDATE. exabgp has one adj-rib-in per neighbour and no view across them, so the
best-match unicast route is the longest unicast prefix this peer announced covering the
flow's destination (after the UPDATE itself is applied). Rule a: exactly one destination
prefix, with offset 0 for IPv6 (RFC 8956 5). Rule b: the same originator, the ORIGINATOR_ID
when there is one. Rule c: no more-specific unicast route inside the destination from
another neighbouring AS (the leftmost AS of its path), which can only differ on iBGP. An
infeasible flow is withheld from the UPDATE and held, capped per family
(IncomingRIB.PENDING_FLOWS_MAX); flow-vpn is not validated. It needs adj-rib-in.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_covered_by_a_shorter_unicast_route_is_feasible`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_flow_specification_without_a_unicast_route_for_its_destination_is_not_feasible`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_unicast_route_for_another_prefix_does_not_make_a_flow_feasible`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_rule_b_a_flow_from_another_originator_than_its_best_match_is_not_feasible`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_rule_c_a_more_specific_route_from_another_neighbouring_as_makes_a_flow_infeasible`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_rule_b_is_judged_against_the_longest_covering_route_not_any_covering_route`
- **7** (SHOULD) `rfc8955#7-additional-actions-specify-interference` - not-applicable
  > Any additional definition of Traffic Filtering Actions SHOULD specify the action to take if those Traffic Filtering Actions interfere (also with existing Traffic Filtering Actions).
  Binds whoever writes the next document defining a traffic filtering action, not whoever
implements this one.  exabgp defines no traffic filtering action of its own; the ones it
knows beyond this document, such as redirect-to-IP, come from other drafts which owe this
sentence themselves.
- **7** (SHOULD) `rfc8955#7-multiple-actions-applied` - not-applicable
  > Multiple Traffic Filtering Actions defined in this document may be present for a single Flow Specification and SHOULD be applied to the traffic flow (for example, traffic-rate-bytes and rt-redirect can be applied to packets at the same time).
  "Applied to the traffic flow" is an instruction to a forwarding plane.  exabgp has none:
it does not manipulate the FIB and no packet ever passes through it, so there is nothing
for an action to be applied to.  What exabgp owes here is to carry every action community
it received to the API without dropping any, and that obligation lives in RFC 4360 and
RFC 7606 rather than in this sentence.
- **7.1** (SHOULD NOT) `rfc8955#7.1-id-not-interpreted` - proven
  > This value is purely informational and SHOULD NOT be interpreted by the implementation.
  The two octet id at the front of a traffic-rate community.  `TrafficRate` exposes it as
`asn` and puts it nowhere else: `__repr__` is `rate-limit:%d` of the rate alone, and no
code branches on it.  The negative side is that an id exabgp would never have chosen,
AS 0 or AS 65535, does not make the community harder to decode or change the rate it
carries.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_two_octet_id_does_not_appear_in_what_we_report`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_an_id_we_would_never_have_chosen_changes_nothing`
- **7.1** (MUST) `rfc8955#7.1-negative-rate-treated-as-zero` - proven
  > On decoding, negative values MUST be treated as zero (discard all traffic).
  `TrafficRate.rate` clamps with `max(value, 0.0)`, as `TrafficRatePackets.rate` does, so a
community carrying -100.0 is reported to the API as `rate-limit:0`, the discard this
sentence defines it to be, rather than as a negative number no consumer can program.  The
negative side is that the clamp is a floor and not a filter: a positive rate comes back
unchanged.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_non_negative_traffic_rate_decodes_unchanged`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_negative_traffic_rate_decodes_as_zero`
- **7.1** (MUST NOT) `rfc8955#7.1-traffic-rate-not-negative-on-encoding` - proven
  > On encoding, the traffic-rate MUST NOT be negative.
  `TrafficRate.make_traffic_rate` raises ValueError on a negative rate, the same check its
sibling `TrafficRatePackets.make_traffic_rate_packets` makes thirty lines lower.  Zero is
legal and means discard everything, so the check is `< 0` and not `<= 0`.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_non_negative_traffic_rate_is_encoded`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_negative_traffic_rate_is_not_encoded`
- **7.2** (MUST) `rfc8955#7.2-negative-rate-packets-treated-as-zero` - proven
  > On decoding, negative values MUST be treated as zero (discard all traffic).
  `TrafficRatePackets.rate` returns `max(value, 0.0)`.  The negative side is that the clamp
is a floor and not a filter: a positive rate must come back unchanged, and -0.0, which is
a negative float that compares equal to zero, must come out as a rate of zero rather than
as anything a consumer would print with a minus sign.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_negative_traffic_rate_packets_decodes_as_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_non_negative_traffic_rate_packets_decodes_unchanged`
- **7.2** (MUST NOT) `rfc8955#7.2-traffic-rate-packets-not-negative-on-encoding` - proven
  > On encoding, the traffic-rate-packets MUST NOT be negative.
  `make_traffic_rate_packets` raises ValueError on a negative rate.  The negative test feeds
it the smallest negative float that exists and the largest positive one, because a check
written as `rate < 0` and a check written as `rate <= 0` differ only at zero, and zero is
a legal traffic-rate-packets meaning discard everything.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_non_negative_traffic_rate_packets_is_encoded`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_a_negative_traffic_rate_packets_is_not_encoded`
- **7.3** (MUST) `rfc8955#7.3-traffic-action-unused-bits-zero` - proven
  > These bits MUST be set to 0 on encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding.
  The only one of this document's four "reserved bits" sentences exabgp gets right in both
directions.  `TrafficAction.make_traffic_action` packs five zero octets and a bitmask
built from the two defined bits, and `sample` and `terminal` read bit 0x02 and bit 0x01 of
the last octet and look at nothing else, so an unused bit set by a peer changes neither.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_traffic_action_we_encode_has_every_unused_bit_at_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_unused_traffic_action_bits_set_by_a_peer_are_ignored`
- **7.5** (MUST) `rfc8955#7.5-traffic-marking-reserved-zero` - proven
  > reserved (r): MUST be set to 0 on encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding
  The encoding half holds because `dscp_value` refuses anything above 63 at configuration
time, so the two reserved bits of the last octet are always zero in what exabgp sends.
The decoding half does not: `TrafficMark.dscp` returns `self._packed[7]`, the whole octet,
so a marking community carrying 0xC1 is reported as `mark 193`.  A DSCP is six bits and
193 is not one; the fix is `& 0x3F` in one place.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_traffic_marking_we_encode_has_its_reserved_bits_at_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8955_flowspec.py::test_the_reserved_bits_of_a_traffic_marking_are_ignored_on_decoding`
- **7.7** (SHOULD) `rfc8955#7.7-document-interference-behaviour` - not-applicable
  > Implementors of this specification SHOULD document the behavior of their implementation in such cases.
  The sentence asks an implementation to document which of two interfering actions it
selects for packet forwarding.  exabgp selects neither: it forwards no packets and applies
no action, and hands every action community it received to the API for the consumer to
resolve.  There is no selection behaviour to write down.
- **9** (SHOULD) `rfc8955#9-traffic-monitoring-facilities` - not-applicable
  > While this is an implementation specific choice, implementations SHOULD provide:
  The two bullets which follow are a mechanism to log the packet header of filtered traffic
and a mechanism to count matches for a flow specification rule.  Both count packets
against a rule exabgp installed, and exabgp installs no rule and sees no packet.  The
counting belongs to whatever the API consumer programmed.

## rfc8956

- **2** (MUST) `rfc8956#2-capability-multiprotocol` - proven
  > Implementations wishing to exchange IPv6 Flow Specifications MUST use BGP's Capability Advertisement facility to exchange the Multiprotocol Extension Capability Code (Code 1), as defined in [RFC4760]. The (AFI, SAFI) pair carried in the Multiprotocol Extension Capability MUST be (AFI=2, SAFI=133) for IPv6 Flow Specification rules and (AFI=2, SAFI=134) for L3VPN Dissemination of Flow Specification rules.
  The IPv6 flow families reuse SAFI 133 and 134, which `Flow` registers under AFI.ipv6 as
well as AFI.ipv4, and they go into the MULTIPROTOCOL capability like any other family.
The negative side is that the capability is a precondition: an MP_REACH_NLRI for 2/133
from a peer which advertised only 1/133 is refused by `MPRNLRI.unpack_attribute`, so an
IPv4 flow session cannot be used to smuggle IPv6 flow specifications in.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_the_ipv6_flow_specification_families_negotiate`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_an_ipv4_flow_session_does_not_carry_ipv6_flow_specifications`
- **3.1** (MUST) `rfc8956#3.1-length-range` - proven
  > If length = 0 and offset = 0, this component matches every address; otherwise, length MUST be in the range offset < length < 129 or the component is malformed.
  Enforced in `IPrefix6.make`, which reads the length and the offset together and refuses
any pair outside `offset < length < 129` unless both are zero.  Length 0 with offset 0
still decodes to ::/0 as the sentence says it should.  Before the check, a component
announcing length 32 and offset 64 was accepted and rendered as 2001:db8::/32/64, a match
on bits 64 to 32 which do not exist.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_a_length_of_zero_with_an_offset_of_zero_matches_every_address`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_a_length_outside_the_range_the_offset_allows_is_refused`
- **3.1** (MUST) `rfc8956#3.1-padding-bits-zero` - proven
  > Padding bits MUST be 0 on encoding and MUST be ignored on decoding.
  Both halves are held by the same pair of helpers, and both halves now hold in general
rather than by luck.  `_pattern_from_address` takes the bits between the offset and the
length and left aligns them, so whatever was written below the length is padded with
zeros rather than sent: `fe80::1/1` goes out as pattern 0x80 where it used to go out as
0xfe, because `IPrefix6.pack` handed `cidr.pack_ip()` straight to the wire and that keeps
the octet exactly as it was stored.  0x80 is the correct reading of "MUST be set to 0 on
encoding": a configuration which sets bits below its own mask has described a prefix that
does not exist, and the bits it set are padding whatever it meant by them.  The old
behaviour only looked compliant because the address in the positive test, 2001:db8::, has
zeros there already.  `_address_from_pattern` does the same in reverse, dropping
everything below the length, so a /33 whose fifth pattern octet is 0xFF decodes to
2001:db8:8000::/33, the same NLRI and the same RIB index as the one padded with zeros.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_the_padding_of_a_prefix_we_encode_is_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_padding_set_by_a_peer_is_ignored_on_decoding`
- **3.3** (SHOULD) `rfc8956#3.3-upper-layer-protocol-single-octet` - proven
  > Type 3 component values SHOULD be encoded as a single octet (numeric_op len=00).
  `FlowNextHeader` derives from `IOperationByte`, whose `encode` always returns one octet,
so the class cannot emit anything wider.  The negative side is the decoder's tolerance,
which is deliberate and not a breach: a peer which sends a next header in two octets is
read rather than refused, because refusing a legal-but-unadvised width dropped real routes
before.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_every_upper_layer_protocol_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_an_upper_layer_protocol_which_would_not_fit_one_octet_cannot_be_encoded`
- **3.4** (SHOULD) `rfc8956#3.4-icmpv6-type-single-octet` - proven
  > Type 7 component values SHOULD be encoded as a single octet (numeric_op len=00).
  `FlowICMPType` derives from `IOperationByte`.  Same shape as type 3 and type 8, and the
three are kept as separate entries because they are three sentences in three sections and
a future change which widened one would be invisible in the other two.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_every_icmpv6_type_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_an_icmpv6_type_which_would_not_fit_one_octet_cannot_be_encoded`
- **3.5** (SHOULD) `rfc8956#3.5-icmpv6-code-single-octet` - proven
  > Type 8 component values SHOULD be encoded as a single octet (numeric_op len=00).
  `FlowICMPCode` derives from `IOperationByte`.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_every_icmpv6_code_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_an_icmpv6_code_which_would_not_fit_one_octet_cannot_be_encoded`
- **3.6** (MUST) `rfc8956#3.6-fragment-reserved-bits-zero` - proven
  > 0: MUST be set to 0 on NLRI encoding and MUST be ignored during decoding
  IPv6 has no Don't Fragment bit, so RFC 8956's fragment bitmask reserves bit 7, the bit
RFC 8955 gives to DF, along with the four high bits.  exabgp shares one `Fragment` class
between the two families and it defines `dont-fragment` at 0x01 unconditionally, so an
IPv6 fragment component carrying 0x01 was reported as `fragment =dont-fragment`: a
reserved bit which should have been ignored was published as a match on a header field
IPv6 does not have.

Type 12 is now split per family the way type 11 already was, into `FlowFragment` for AFI 1
and `FlowFragmentIPv6` for AFI 2, because the set of bits the family defines is the one
thing about the component which differs and the decoder is where it has to be applied.
The IPv6 decoder masks with IsF, FF and LF only, so 0x01 decodes to nothing at all.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_the_four_high_bits_of_an_ipv6_fragment_bitmask_we_encode_are_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_the_reserved_bits_of_an_ipv6_fragment_bitmask_are_ignored_on_decoding`
- **3.6** (MUST) `rfc8956#3.6-fragment-single-octet` - proven
  > The Type 12 component bitmask MUST be encoded as a single octet bitmask (bitmask_op len=00).
  `FlowFragmentIPv6`, the sibling of IPv4's `FlowFragment`, so the same accidental
compliance: it derives from `IOperationByteShort`, which would emit two octets above 255,
and what keeps it to one is that no name `Fragment` defines is above 0x0F.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_every_ipv6_fragment_bitmask_we_encode_uses_a_single_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_an_ipv6_fragment_component_of_two_octets_is_not_what_we_generate`
- **3.7** (SHOULD) `rfc8956#3.7-flow-label-four-octets` - proven
  > Type 13 component values SHOULD be encoded as 4-octet quantities (numeric_op len=10).
  `FlowFlowLabel` encodes through `IOperationLong`, four octets whatever the value; on
receipt any width the operator octet allows is still read.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc8956_flowspec_ipv6.py::test_every_flow_label_we_encode_uses_four_octets`

## rfc9003

- **2** (MUST) `rfc9003#2-communication-is-utf8` - proven
  > To support international characters, the Shutdown Communication field MUST be encoded using UTF-8.
  Positive only: this constrains what we send.  The receiving side is the next entry.
Notify encoded the text as ASCII, so a communication with one accented letter raised
UnicodeEncodeError rather than being sent.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_the_communication_is_encoded_in_utf8`
- **2** (MAY) `rfc9003#2-may-include-a-communication` - untested (missing: positive)
  > If a BGP speaker decides to terminate its session with a BGP neighbor, and it sends a NOTIFICATION message with the Error Code "Cease" and Error Subcode "Administrative Shutdown" or "Administrative Reset" [RFC4486], it MAY include a UTF-8-encoded string.
  Notify(6, 2, text) and Notify(6, 4, text) carry the text as the communication.  exabgp's
own teardown sends none, which the RFC allows and which every receiver understands.
- **2** (MUST NOT) `rfc9003#2-must-not-interpret-invalid-utf8` - proven
  > A receiving BGP speaker MUST NOT interpret invalid UTF-8 sequences.
  Notification.text reports an invalid sequence as invalid, with the octets in hex, rather
than decoding it with replacement characters (Notification.data is the field as it was on
the wire).  A valid communication is shown, not interpreted either: a control character
in it becomes a space.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_valid_utf8_is_interpreted`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_invalid_utf8_is_reported_and_not_interpreted`
- **2** (SHOULD) `rfc9003#2-report-the-communication` - proven
  > Mechanisms concerning the reporting of information contained in the Shutdown Communication are implementation specific but SHOULD include methods such as syslog [RFC5424].
  A received NOTIFICATION goes to the log, which can be syslog, and to the API as
`notification` with the decoded communication.  The log line is str(Notification), which
decoded the communication as ASCII and so showed any non-ASCII one in hex; it is UTF-8.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_a_utf8_communication_is_logged_as_text_not_hex`
- **2** (REQUIRED) `rfc9003#2-shortest-form-required` - proven
  > UTF-8 "Shortest Form" encoding is REQUIRED to guard against the technical issues outlined in [UTR36].
  Python's UTF-8 codec produces the shortest form and refuses an overlong one, so both sides
come from the codec; the tests pin that it is the strict codec in use.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_what_we_send_is_shortest_form`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_an_overlong_encoding_is_not_accepted`
- **2** (MUST) `rfc9003#2-subcode-is-shutdown-or-reset` - proven
  > Subcode: The Error Subcode value MUST be one of the following values: 2 ("Administrative Shutdown") or 4 ("Administrative Reset").
  Only (6, 2) and (6, 4) get the length octet on send, and only those are parsed as a
communication on receipt.  The negative test is another Cease subcode, whose text goes out
as it is.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_shutdown_and_reset_carry_a_length_prefixed_communication`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_another_cease_subcode_carries_no_length_octet`
- **3** (SHOULD NOT) `rfc9003#3-no-longer-than-128-octets-unless-known` - proven
  > Otherwise, a Shutdown Communication MAY be sent, but it SHOULD NOT be longer than 128 octets.
  Notify cuts the communication to 128 octets, on a character boundary so what is sent is
still valid UTF-8.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_a_long_communication_is_cut_to_128_octets_on_a_character_boundary`
- **3** (MAY) `rfc9003#3-up-to-255-octets-may-be-sent` - proven
  > If it is known that the peer BGP speaker supports this specification, then a Shutdown Communication that is not longer than 255 octets MAY be sent.
  exabgp cannot know it, so it never sends more than 128.  What this obliges is the other
side: a peer which knows we support RFC 9003 may send 255, and we must read it.  We read
anything up to the 255 the one octet Length field can say.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9003_shutdown_communication.py::test_a_communication_longer_than_128_octets_is_accepted`
- **4** (MAY) `rfc9003#4-hexdump-malformed` - untested (missing: positive)
  > An erroneous or malformed Shutdown Communication itself MAY be logged in a hexdump format.
  The invalid rendering carries the octets in hex after the reason.
- **4** (SHOULD) `rfc9003#4-log-invalid-utf8` - untested (missing: positive)
  > If a Shutdown Communication with an invalid UTF-8 sequence is received, a message indicating this event SHOULD be logged for the attention of the operator.
  The rendering says "invalid Shutdown Communication (invalid UTF-8)", and that rendering is
what the received NOTIFICATION is logged as.

## rfc9012

- **11** (MUST) `rfc9012#11-must-be-able-to-filter-incoming` - proven
  > To prevent the Tunnel Encapsulation attribute from being distributed beyond its intended scope, any BGP speaker that understands the attribute MUST be able to filter the attribute from incoming BGP UPDATE messages. When the attribute is filtered from an incoming UPDATE, the attribute is neither processed nor distributed.
  The neighbour option `tunnel-encapsulation <auto | filter | accept>;` filters the
attribute from incoming UPDATEs per session.  `auto`, the default, filters it from every
EBGP neighbour, as the rest of section 11 requires, and keeps it from a neighbour inside
the AS or the confederation.  A filtered attribute is removed by `AttributeCollection.parse`
before it is decoded, so it is neither processed nor handed to the API, and the route
itself is still accepted.  The tests turn the filter on by default for EBGP and by option
for IBGP, and show the attribute kept over IBGP by default and wherever `accept` is set.
Filtering on the way out, and filtering the Encapsulation Extended Community, are not
done; neither is in this ledger entry.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_ebgp_session_filters_the_tunnel_encapsulation_attribute_from_an_incoming_update`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_the_filter_can_be_turned_on_for_an_ibgp_session`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_the_tunnel_encapsulation_attribute_is_kept_where_it_is_not_filtered`
- **13** (MUST) `rfc9012#13-duplicate-subtlv-first-wins` - proven
  > If a Tunnel TLV has more than one of any of these sub-TLVs, all but the first occurrence of each such sub-TLV type MUST be disregarded. However, the Tunnel TLV containing them MUST NOT be considered to be malformed, and all the sub-TLVs MUST be propagated if the route carrying the Tunnel Encapsulation attribute is propagated.
  The two halves pull in opposite directions and are implemented in different places.
`SubTLV.unpack_subtlvs` still appends every sub-TLV it walks and `pack_value` still emits
every one of them, which is "MUST NOT be considered malformed" and "all the sub-TLVs MUST
be propagated".  "Disregarded" is applied in `SRPolicyTunnel.json`, which emits the first
occurrence only of each sub-TLV type RFC 9830 2.4 says must not repeat: Preference,
Binding SID, ENLP, Priority, and the two name sub-TLVs.  exabgp installs nothing, so the
only thing it can be said to interpret is what it hands the API, and the JSON is that.
Segment List and SRv6 Binding SID are not collapsed, RFC 9830 lets both repeat.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_repeated_preference_subtlv_keeps_only_the_first`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_the_disregarded_preference_is_still_on_the_wire_we_re_advertise`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_repeated_preference_subtlv_does_not_make_the_tlv_malformed`
- **13** (MUST) `rfc9012#13-malformed-subtlv-as-unrecognized` - proven
  > In general, if a TLV contains a sub-TLV that is malformed, the sub- TLV MUST be treated as if it were an unrecognized sub-TLV.
  Two kinds of malformation, answered in two places.  A sub-TLV whose *framing* is wrong - a
one byte tail, a length which runs past the end of the enclosing Tunnel TLV, a type above
127 with fewer than three bytes left - still raises `Notify(3, 1)` out of
`SubTLV.unpack_subtlvs`, which `TunnelEncap.TREAT_AS_WITHDRAW` turns into treat-as-withdraw
rather than the NOTIFICATION it used to be.  That is stricter than this sentence asks, and
it is what the walk can honestly do: with the framing broken there is no way to know where
the sub-TLV ends and so no way to skip just it.  A sub-TLV whose framing is sound and whose
*value* the decoder refuses is the case this sentence really covers, and it is now handled
as written: the decoder raises `MalformedSubTLV` and `unpack_subtlvs` keeps the bytes as a
`GenericSubTLV`, out of the interpretation and into what we propagate.
`PreferenceSubTLV.unpack` is the first to do it; it used to answer `cls(0)` and tell the
API the peer had asked for preference zero.  The exception this section makes for a
malformed Tunnel Egress Endpoint sub-TLV does not arise: exabgp registers no decoder for
that sub-TLV, so it can never find one malformed.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_one_byte_subtlv_tail_does_not_reset_the_session`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_subtlv_above_127_with_a_truncated_length_field_does_not_reset_the_session`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_short_preference_subtlv_is_read_as_unrecognised_not_as_zero`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_malformed_preference_does_not_take_the_sub_tlvs_beside_it`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_sr_policy_subtlv_of_the_wrong_length_is_read_as_unrecognised`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_well_formed_preference_subtlv_is_still_read_as_a_preference`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_malformed_subtlv_no_longer_ends_the_session`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_sr_policy_subtlv_of_a_length_rfc_9830_gives_is_read`
- **13** (MUST) `rfc9012#13-no-valid-tlv-or-not-transitive` - proven
  > If a Tunnel Encapsulation attribute does not have any valid TLVs, or it does not have the transitive bit set, the "Treat-as-withdraw" procedure of [RFC7606] is applied.
  Both halves give treat-as-withdraw.  `TunnelEncap` declares `TREAT_AS_WITHDRAW` and not
`VALID_ZERO`, so in `AttributeCollection.parse` an empty attribute (no TLV at all) is
withdrawn by the zero length rule in `_add_registered`, and an attribute sent optional
but not transitive fails the `Attribute.registered(aid, flag)` lookup and is withdrawn by
the `aid in Attribute.attributes_known` branch.  `UpdateCollection` then moves every
announced prefix of the UPDATE to its withdrawals, which the tests check end to end.

On an EBGP session left at its default, section 11 has the attribute filtered on receipt
before its flags are read: a filtered attribute is "neither processed nor distributed",
so it withdraws nothing there.  The tests use an IBGP session for that reason.

An attribute whose only TLVs are of unrecognised tunnel types is not counted as having
no valid TLV: the next paragraph says such a TLV is not malformed and must be kept for
propagation, and `rfc9012#13-unknown-tunnel-type-not-malformed` tests that.

This entry carries a "MUST" level because the "Treat-as-withdraw" procedure it invokes is
normative in RFC 7606; the sentence itself states the consequence rather than a keyword.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_empty_tunnel_encapsulation_attribute_is_treated_as_withdraw`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_attribute_without_the_transitive_bit_is_treated_as_withdraw`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_route_carrying_a_non_transitive_tunnel_encapsulation_is_withdrawn`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_route_carrying_an_empty_tunnel_encapsulation_is_withdrawn`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_optional_transitive_attribute_with_one_tlv_is_not_withdrawn`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_route_carrying_a_valid_transitive_tunnel_encapsulation_is_announced`
- **13** (MUST) `rfc9012#13-tlv-ends-with-final-subtlv` - proven
  > The final octet of a TLV MUST also be the final octet of its final sub-TLV. If this is not the case, the TLV MUST be considered to be malformed, and the "Treat-as- withdraw" procedure of [RFC7606] is applied.
  `SubTLV.walk_subtlvs` raises `Notify(3, 1)` when a sub-TLV length does not land exactly on
the end of its Tunnel TLV, and `TunnelEncap.TREAT_AS_WITHDRAW` turns that into the
treat-as-withdraw this sentence asks for rather than the NOTIFICATION it used to become.

The rule used to be enforced only where a tunnel type decoder happened to exist, which was
tunnel type 15 and nothing else: `TunnelTypeTLV.unpack_tunnel` handed every other type to
`GenericTunnelTLV`, which keeps the value as opaque bytes, so a TLV whose final sub-TLV
ended early or late was accepted without complaint.  `unpack_tunnel` now walks the chain
for an unregistered type too.  The walk is framing only, and deliberately so: the same
section says an unrecognised sub-TLV "MUST remain in the attribute" when the route is
propagated, so nothing is read out of one and the TLV is still kept as the peer's bytes.
Splitting the walk out of `unpack_subtlvs` is what keeps the two callers on one set of
length checks instead of two that could drift apart.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_subtlv_running_past_the_end_of_its_tlv_withdraws_rather_than_resets`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_short_final_subtlv_is_caught_in_an_unrecognised_tunnel_type_too`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_subtlv_above_127_ending_early_is_caught_in_an_unrecognised_tunnel_type_too`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_tlv_whose_final_subtlv_ends_exactly_at_its_end_is_accepted`
- **13** (MUST NOT) `rfc9012#13-unknown-tunnel-type-not-malformed` - proven
  > If a Tunnel Encapsulation attribute can be parsed correctly but contains a TLV whose tunnel type is not recognized by a particular BGP speaker, that BGP speaker MUST NOT consider the attribute to be malformed. Rather, it MUST interpret the attribute as if that TLV had not been present. If the route carrying the Tunnel Encapsulation attribute is propagated with the attribute, the unrecognized TLV MUST remain in the attribute.
  `TunnelTypeTLV.unpack_tunnel` falls back to `GenericTunnelTLV`, which keeps the tunnel
type number and the raw value and re-emits both from `pack()`.  Tested from both sides:
an attribute made only of unrecognised tunnel types decodes without error, and the bytes
it packs back are the bytes the peer sent.  The negative test is the one which would
catch a decoder that dropped the unknown TLV on re-encode, which is the mistake this
sentence exists to forbid.  The third test is the boundary with the entry above: the
framing walk `unpack_tunnel` now runs over an unrecognised type checks lengths and stops,
so a sub-TLV type this implementation can decode, sitting inside a tunnel type it cannot,
still comes back out as opaque bytes rather than as a decoded preference.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_attribute_of_nothing_but_an_unrecognised_tunnel_type_decodes`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_walking_an_unrecognised_tunnel_type_does_not_start_reading_its_sub_tlvs`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_unrecognised_tunnel_type_is_not_dropped_when_the_attribute_is_re_packed`
- **13** (MUST) `rfc9012#13-unrecognized-subtlv-ignored-and-kept` - proven
  > If a TLV of a Tunnel Encapsulation attribute contains a sub-TLV that is not recognized by a particular BGP speaker, the BGP speaker MUST process that TLV as if the unrecognized sub-TLV had not been present. If the route carrying the Tunnel Encapsulation attribute is propagated with the attribute, the unrecognized sub-TLV MUST remain in the attribute.
  `SubTLV.unpack_subtlvs` wraps an unregistered sub-TLV type in `GenericSubTLV`, which
keeps the raw value and re-emits it with the right one or two octet length field for its
type number.  The negative side is the re-encode: a `GenericSubTLV` with a type above
127 must come back with a two octet length, and one below 128 with one octet, or the
sub-TLV that "remains in the attribute" is not the one that arrived.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_unrecognised_subtlv_does_not_stop_the_tlv_being_processed`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_a_run_of_zero_length_tlvs_and_subtlvs_terminates`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9012_tunnel_encap.py::test_an_unrecognised_subtlv_above_127_keeps_its_two_octet_length_on_re_encode`

## rfc9072

- **2** (MUST) `rfc9072#2-accept-extended-encoding-of-short-parameters` - proven
  > (In any case, an implementation MUST accept an OPEN message that uses the encoding of this specification even if the length of the Optional Parameters is 255 or less.)
  The negative test is an extended length which runs past the parameters sent: accepting
the encoding is not accepting a length the bytes do not back.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_the_extended_encoding_of_a_few_octets_is_accepted`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_an_extended_length_beyond_the_parameters_is_refused`
- **2** (MUST) `rfc9072#2-encode-extended-beyond-255` - proven
  > However, if the length of the Optional Parameters in the BGP OPEN message does exceed 255, the OPEN message MUST be encoded according to the procedure below.
  pack_capabilities keeps the RFC 4271 encoding while the one Capabilities parameter fits in
255 octets, the SHOULD before this sentence, and uses the extended one past that.  The
negative test is a short set, which stays in the RFC 4271 encoding.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_more_than_255_octets_go_out_in_the_extended_encoding`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_255_octets_or_fewer_go_out_in_the_rfc_4271_encoding`
- **2** (MUST NOT) `rfc9072#2-non-ext-op-len-not-zero` - proven
  > The Non-Extended Optional Parameters Length field (Non-Ext OP Len.) SHOULD be set to 255 on transmission and, in any event, MUST NOT be set to 0; it MUST be ignored on receipt once the use of the extended format is determined positively by inspection of the Non-Extended Optional Parameters Type (Non-Ext OP Type) field.
  We send 255.  On receipt the value is ignored once the octet after it is 255, which until
2026-10-08 was the other way round: only a length octet of 255 was looked at, and any
other value had the OPEN read as a parameter of type 255 and refused with 2/4.  The
negative test is a peer which sets it to 0: that is no Optional Parameters at all, as the
procedure below only inspects the next octet when the length is non-zero.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_more_than_255_octets_go_out_in_the_extended_encoding`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_a_length_of_zero_is_no_optional_parameters_even_before_type_255`
- **2** (MUST) `rfc9072#2-non-ext-op-type-255-on-transmission` - proven
  > The subsequent one-octet field (which would be the first Optional Parameter Type field in the non-extended format and is called "Non- Ext OP Type" in the figure above) MUST be set to 255 on transmission.
  Positive only: it constrains what we send.  What a received one means is the next entry.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_more_than_255_octets_go_out_in_the_extended_encoding`
- **2** (MUST) `rfc9072#2-non-ext-op-type-decides-the-encoding` - proven
  > In parsing an OPEN message, if the one-octet Optional Parameters Length field (labeled "Non-Ext OP Len." in Figure 1) is non-zero, a BGP speaker MUST use the value of the octet following the one-octet Optional Parameters Length field (labeled "Non-Ext OP Type" in Figure 1) to determine both the encoding of the Optional Parameters length and the size of the Parameter Length field of individual Optional Parameters.
  Capabilities.unpack decodes the extended encoding when that octet is 255, whatever the
length octet before it; until 2026-10-08 it looked at the length octet only.  The negative
test is a length octet of 255 followed by an ordinary parameter type, which is the RFC
4271 encoding.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_a_first_type_of_255_is_the_extended_encoding_whatever_the_length_octet`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_a_length_of_255_with_another_first_type_is_the_rfc_4271_encoding`
- **3** (MUST) `rfc9072#3-type-255-after-another-length-is-extended` - proven
  > It is not considered a fatal error to receive an OPEN message whose (non-extended) Optional Parameters Length value is not 255 and whose first Optional Parameter type code is 255 -- in this case, the encoding of this specification MUST be used for decoding the message.
  The finding of rfc9072#2-non-ext-op-type-decides-the-encoding, seen from section 3: until
2026-10-08 such an OPEN was refused with 2/4.  Positive only: the case is defined by the
peer's input, and the input outside it, a length of 255 with another first type, is the
negative test of that entry.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_a_first_type_of_255_is_the_extended_encoding_whatever_the_length_octet`
- **3** (MUST) `rfc9072#3-type-255-elsewhere-is-unrecognised` - proven
  > If encountered other than as the Non-Ext OP Type, it MUST be treated as an unrecognized Optional Parameter and handled according to [RFC4271], Section 6.2.
  Type 255 as a later parameter, in either encoding, is refused with Unsupported Optional
Parameters (2/4), as any parameter type we do not know.  The negative test is 255 as the
Non-Ext OP Type, which is not a parameter.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_type_255_anywhere_else_is_an_unsupported_optional_parameter`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9072_extended_optional_parameters.py::test_type_255_as_the_non_ext_op_type_is_not_a_parameter`

## rfc9085

- **2.1.1** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.1.1-sid-label-length` - proven
  > Type: 1161 Length: Variable. Either 3 or 4 octets depending on whether the value is encoded as a label or as an index/SID.
  Held where the SID/Label TLV is a sub-TLV of the SR Capabilities TLV (2.1.2):
`SrCapabilities.unpack_bgpls` refuses one of any other size, which the `sids` property used
to step over without a word, so the range it carried left the process with no trace.  A
four octet SID, which 2.1.2 says is not valid there, is a value and not a size, and RFC
9552 8.2.2 forbids calling the attribute malformed over a value: it is still decoded.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_sr_capabilities_carry_a_label_range`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_a_four_octet_sid_is_a_value_and_does_not_make_the_tlv_malformed`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_a_sid_label_of_another_size_is_refused_rather_than_stepped_over`
- **2.2.1** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.2.1-adjacency-sid-flags-per-protocol` - known gap, demonstrated by a failing test
  > Flags: 1-octet value that should be set as: * IS-IS Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 2.2.1 of [RFC8667]. * OSPFv2 Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 6.1 of [RFC8665]. * OSPFv3 Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 7.1 of [RFC8666].
  Read with the IS-IS layout whatever the Protocol-ID of the Link NLRI, which the attribute
decoder cannot see (see the header): an OSPF label, V and L set (0x60), is reported as
B and V.  The SID itself is decoded from its size, so it is no longer lost.  Doing this
would mean decoding the attribute against the NLRI it arrived with, after both are read,
and keeping that decision out of the per-session attribute cache.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_an_ospf_adjacency_label_is_reported_with_the_ospf_flags`
- **2.2.2** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.2.2-lan-adjacency-sid-flags-per-protocol` - known gap, demonstrated by a failing test
  > Flags: 1-octet value that should be set as: * IS-IS LAN Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8667]. * OSPFv2 LAN Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 6.2 of [RFC8665]. * OSPFv3 LAN Adj-SID flags as defined in Section 7.2 of [RFC8666].
  The same gap as 2.2.1-adjacency-sid-flags-per-protocol.  The Neighbor ID, which this
section also gives the layout of the IGP, is read from the length: 11 or 12 octets are
OSPF, with a four octet Router-ID, where it used to be read as a six octet IS-IS System
ID which took two octets of the SID with it.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_an_ospf_lan_adjacency_label_is_reported_with_the_ospf_flags`
- **2.3.1** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.3.1-prefix-sid-flags-per-protocol` - known gap, demonstrated by a failing test
  > The Flags and, as an extension, the SID/Index/Label fields of this TLV are interpreted according to the respective underlying IS-IS, OSPFv2, or OSPFv3 protocol. The Protocol-ID of the BGP-LS Prefix NLRI is used to determine the underlying protocol specification for parsing these fields.
  The same gap as 2.2.1-adjacency-sid-flags-per-protocol: the attribute decoder cannot see
the Protocol-ID, and the flags are named with the IS-IS layout (R, N, P, E, V, L).  An
OSPF NP-Flag (0x40) is reported as N, and an OSPF M-Flag (0x20) as P.  V and L share
their bits between the layouts, so the SID itself is read correctly.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_an_ospf_prefix_sid_is_reported_with_the_ospf_flags`
- **2.3.1** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.3.1-prefix-sid-length` - proven
  > Length: Variable. 7 or 8 octets depending on the label or index encoding of the SID.
  Seven octets with V and L set read as a label, eight with neither as an index, and an
index of any other size is refused, since nothing can be read from it.  A label TLV of
another size is kept, as RFC 9552 8.2.2 asks a propagator not to judge the length of a
TLV (`qa/bin/compat_gate` counts every such size a release accepted): what follows the
last whole label is reported in `undecoded-sids`.  It used to be dropped without a trace,
so a four octet value behind V and L lost its last octet.  V and L are the same two bits
in the IS-IS (RFC 8667 2.1.1) and OSPF (RFC 8665 5) layouts, so none of this needs the
Protocol-ID.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_a_prefix_sid_of_seven_or_eight_octets_is_read`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_a_label_prefix_sid_of_another_size_keeps_what_follows_the_label`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_an_index_prefix_sid_of_another_size_is_refused`
- **2.3.2** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.3.2-prefix-attribute-flags-per-protocol` - known gap, demonstrated by a failing test
  > The Flags field of this TLV is interpreted according to the respective underlying IS-IS, OSPFv2, or OSPFv3 protocol. The Protocol-ID of the BGP-LS Prefix NLRI is used to determine the underlying protocol specification for parsing this field.
  The same gap as 2.2.1-adjacency-sid-flags-per-protocol: the attribute decoder cannot see
the Protocol-ID, and the flags are named with the IS-IS layout of RFC 7794 (X, R, N).  The
OSPFv2 A-Flag and N-Flag of RFC 7684 2.1 (0x80, 0x40) are reported as X and R, and the
OSPFv3 Prefix Options of RFC 5340 A.4.1.1 under the same three names.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_ospfv2_prefix_attribute_flags_are_reported_with_the_ospf_flags`
- **2.3.2** (SHOULD) `rfc9085#2.3.2-prefix-attribute-flags-variable-length` - proven
  > Type: 1170 Length: Variable. Flags: a variable-length Flag field (according to the Length field).
  `PrefixAttributesFlags` reads the flags it knows from the first octet and keeps any octet
after it, rendered in hex as `undecoded-flags`.  It declared a length of exactly one octet,
so a longer field was a wrong size, and RFC 9552 8.2.2 discarded the whole attribute with
every other TLV in it.  The negative side: an empty field has no flags to read.
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_prefix_attribute_flags_longer_than_one_octet_are_kept`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_one_octet_prefix_attribute_flags_render_as_they_always_did`
  - `tests/unit/rfc/test_rfc9085_bgpls_sr.py::test_empty_prefix_attribute_flags_are_refused`

## rfc9136

- **3** (MUST) `rfc9136#3-nves-must-support-rt5` - not-applicable
